--- 2026-03-01 | Deeva Cintron <-> Daniel ---
[19:14] Daniel: Marco found a genealogy record for an Otto Meyer who arrived at Baltimore in 1908 — age 22, from the Grand Duchy of Baden. Could that be your grandfather?
[19:22] Deeva: That would be him. Grandpa Otto always said he came from a town near Freiburg. The dates fit — he'd have been young, maybe 21 or 22. He settled in Toledo first then moved to the Adrian area for factory work.
[19:25] Daniel: It says occupation 'laborer.' Makes sense.
[19:29] Deeva: He was a union man. UAW later, once the plants organized. Grandpa Otto believed very strongly in that. Father followed him into it.
[19:31] Daniel: The labor roots run deep. I love it.
[19:35] Daniel: Did your father speak German at all?
[19:41] Deeva: He understood it — Grandpa Otto spoke it at home when Father was small. But Father stopped speaking it around 1942 or 1943. He and his brothers just stopped. Nobody discussed it much at the time. It was the war.
[19:44] Daniel: That happened in so many German-American families. Did Grandpa Otto keep speaking it?
[19:48] Deeva: Not in public. Maybe a word or two at home privately. By the time I was born in 1940 it was just gone from the household. There was nothing left of it. I never heard a sentence of German in our house growing up.

[19:40] Daniel: And his schooling?
[19:44] Deeva: He left school in eighth grade. That was common for his generation in that town. He was sharp as anyone I've met but he had to work from young. It's one of the reasons he pushed me toward business school — he wanted more for his children.

[16:41] Kathy: So we carry the name but not the culture, in a way.

[16:17] Deeva: I'll tell her when we talk Sunday. At sixteen I was living at home in Adrian, both parents were married and working, Maggie and the boys were all home too. It was 1956 and we had the same house we'd had since I was small. Nothing dramatic to report, which was itself a kind of gift.
[16:19] Kathy: She will love that. The normalcy of it.
[16:21] Kathy: Were you close to your grandmother when you were young?
[16:27] Deeva: Very close. Grandma Elsa lived with us from about 1953 until 1961 when she passed. She had her own room off the kitchen. She taught me to knit and to keep a neat account book. I was thirteen when she moved in and I spent a lot of time with her in the evenings.
[16:29] Kathy: That's a long time to share a house.
[16:32] Deeva: Eight years. It shaped how I thought about taking care of elders. You keep them close if you can. Maggie and I both got that lesson from Grandma Elsa being there.
[16:35] Kathy: When did you lose your dad, Mom? Emma wants to include that for Grandpa Meyer's entry.
[16:41] Deeva: 1974. I was thirty-four. Kathy you were twelve. He died in March — I was just beginning that night class at Washtenaw. It hit hard because there was no warning. He'd retired from Bohn in 1969 and had five good years, then gone.
[16:43] Kathy: I remember the house being very quiet for a long time after.
[16:46] Deeva: It was. Mother had been expecting more years. We all had.
